**CLI.** `infrawrench page <message> --source …`, `infrawrench page clear`, and `infrawrench costs push --source … [--file|stdin]` (`desktop/electron/cli/commands/push.ts`) wrap both endpoints — the CLI is usually already on the box that has the news. Suppressed pages exit zero and print the retry time.

Docs: `website/src/content/docs/features/server-push.md` (+ cloud-costs, workflows, cli, push/Slack/Teams alert triggers, roles, api-keys).

## Org config as code (cloud-only)

One JSON document carrying everything an org configures that is neither a provider account nor a live resource: dashboards (with their cards), workflows, custom graphs, budgets, metric alert rules, synthetic probes, cost centres + allocation rules, the tag policy, and the five org-wide alert-settings rows. Export is a read fan-out; apply is the same writers in one transaction. Verb pair: `config export` / `config plan` / `config apply`.

- **Keys, not ids.** Every entity is addressed by a slug derived from its name (`uniqueOrgConfigKey`), never by a row id — that is what makes one document apply to a staging org, a fresh DR org, and the org it came from. Cross-references are rewritten on export and resolved on apply: a budget widget's `config.budgetId` becomes `budgetKey`, a `custom_graph` widget's `graphId` becomes `graphKey`, a workflow's budget trigger carries `budgetKey`, an allocation rule's `accountId` and the drift scope carry account **display names**. `client-core/src/org-config.ts` holds the document + plan types, the section→permission tables, `ORG_CONFIG_KEY_PATTERN`, and the key derivation; the zod schema lives in `web/src/services/org-config/schema.ts` with instantiated `Exact<>` assertions against those types (**instantiated** — a bare `type X = [Exact<A,B>]` alias resolves to `never` without erroring, so the existing `SchemasMatchCostContract`-style guards only bite when something assigns `true` to them).
- **Key derivation must run over a stable order.** `state.ts` orders every collection by `createdAt, id` — deliberately NOT the order the list endpoints use. `listWorkflows` sorts by `updatedAt`, so deriving keys from that would rename every key whenever someone saved a workflow, and the next apply would delete-and-recreate the lot.
- **One state loader for both directions.** `loadOrgConfigState(orgId)` returns each row already rendered in document shape, so export serialises it straight out and apply diffs the incoming document against it. A round trip is only lossless if "what we have" and "what a document says" are computed by the same code — which is also what makes an unchanged document report as entirely `unchanged` rather than rewriting everything.
- **Three verbs, `web/src/api/routes/org-config.ts`** — `GET /config/export` (`?sections=` narrows), `POST /config/plan` (the dry run; read-only, so a PR reviewer can run it), `POST /config/apply`. New permissions `config:read` / `config:write`, **plus** the per-section permission of every section involved — `config:write` alone would otherwise be a way around a role that deliberately withholds `workflows:write`, since a document carries workflow source. Export refuses rather than omitting a section the caller can't read: a partial document looks complete, and applying it with `replace` would delete everything the exporter couldn't see. Admin gets both through `ALL_EXCEPT`; Member gets neither.
- **Plan then execute.** `buildOrgConfigPlan` resolves every reference and queues closures (`(tx) => Promise<void>`) while nothing is written; ids for creates are minted during planning so later sections can point at them. `applyOrgConfig` then runs the whole queue inside one `db.transaction`. This is why the writes are direct `tx` calls rather than calls into `services/budgets.ts` et al — those helpers close over the module-level `db` and can't join a transaction. The validators are still shared (`budgetInputSchema`'s threshold/filter schemas, `widgetConfigSchemaFor`, `normalizeTagPolicy`, `normalizeAnomalySettings`, `normalizeProbeUrl`, `PROBE_LIMITS`, `METRIC_ALERT_LIMITS`, `DRIFT_ALERT_LIMITS`, `EXPIRY_ALERT_LIMITS`), so a config document can never smuggle in a shape the hand-editing route would refuse. `EXPIRY_ALERT_LIMITS` moved to `client-core/src/expiry.ts` and is re-exported from `server-core/expiry/settings.ts` in the process; `DRIFT_ALERT_LIMITS` already had a client-core copy.
- **`merge` vs `replace`.** Merge creates and updates what the document names. Replace additionally deletes what it doesn't — but only **within the sections the document carries**, so a budgets-only file can never touch workflows whichever mode it runs in. The default dashboard is never deleted (the hand-editing route refuses too); that shows up as an `unresolved` entry rather than a silent skip.
- **`unresolved` is not an error.** A resource pin nobody has synced, an account name this org hasn't connected, a workflow whose budget trigger names a budget the document doesn't define — each is reported and the affected card/clause/deletion is dropped, so a restore is not held hostage by one dangling pin. Two things fail loudly instead, because guessing is worse: a drift scope that resolves to zero accounts (empty means _every_ account, so dropping unknown names would widen alerting), and a widget/probe the normal editor would also have rejected.
- **What is deliberately out.** Accounts and credentials (an export must never be a credential leak), resources, workflow webhook **signing** secrets (write-only by contract — the read path redacts them, so no document can carry one), and every claim/bookkeeping column: `lastNotifiedAt`, `lastSentWeekStart`, `smsLastPagedAt`, firing history, run logs, change freezes. Restoring a cooldown claim from a `git push` would re-open a quiet period and page people twice. Enabling the digest through a document sets `lastSentWeekStart` to the current window, exactly as `updateOrgDigestSettings` does, so an apply can't fire a digest immediately.
- **Dashboard cards are anonymous and ordered**, so the only sound update is to replace the whole set — done only when the card list actually differs, so an unchanged document doesn't churn widget ids on every apply. Same rule for a cost centre's allocation rules. `gridX` is the index in the one sequence all three card tables share (see the dashboard card-order section above).
- **Surfaces** — CLI `infrawrench config export|plan|apply` (`desktop/electron/cli/commands/config.ts`; `--out`, `--sections`, `--prune`, `-y`; apply always plans first, even with `-y`, so an unattended run leaves a record of what it did, and refuses to apply unconfirmed on a non-TTY). Shared settings section `ui/src/settings/ConfigAsCodeSection.tsx` (registry key `config`, permission-gated on `config:read`) — the preview is not optional there either. Desktop reaches it through the existing `cloud_settings_request` allowlist (`config` added to the org alternation). **Mobile is a deliberate omission**, alongside the Monaco editors and the anomaly-tuning knobs: this is a git-workflow surface whose input is a file.
- OpenAPI `paths/org-config.ts`; API_VERSION 0.38.0 → **0.39.0**. Docs: `website/src/content/docs/features/config-as-code.md` (+ cli.md, roles-and-permissions.md).

## IaC reconciliation — the ClickOps detector (cloud-only)

An org uploads the Terraform state it already has; every synced resource is classified **managed / drifted / unmanaged**, and the unmanaged ones become `import` blocks. `Plugin.terraformExport` was one-way (inventory → HCL); this is the inverse read.

**Naming, because there are now four.** Four features in this repo have "Terraform" in the name, and each entry below already had to disambiguate against the other three — so, once, in a table:

| Feature                | Direction               | Unit                | Where                                          |
| ---------------------- | ----------------------- | ------------------- | ---------------------------------------------- |
| **IaC reconciliation** | Terraform state → in    | user's cloud rsrcs  | `client-core/src/iac/`, `server-core/src/iac/` |
| eject to Terraform     | HCL → out               | user's cloud rsrcs  | `plugin-base/terraform*.ts`                    |
| org config as code     | one JSON doc, both ways | the Infrawrench org | `web/src/services/org-config/`                 |
| Terraform provider     | Terraform → Infrawrench | the Infrawrench org | `terraform-provider-infrawrench/`              |

Call this one **IaC reconciliation** in prose and `iac` in code. It is the only one that reads state _in_. It shares the mappers with eject-to-Terraform and nothing at all with the other two.

- **The reverse mapping is derived, never written twice.** `client-core/src/iac/type-map.ts` `deriveTerraformTypeMap` probes each plugin's own `terraformExport.mapResource` with a `Proxy`-backed `fields` bag (four probe values — a string, `"1"`, `"true"`, `"[]"` — so mappers that branch on a value are covered, unioned) and reads the block type back. A plugin that gains export support gains reconciliation for free, and the two directions cannot drift apart. `ownKeys` on the proxy stays **empty** on purpose: a mapper that iterates the bag (tags, labels) must see nothing rather than an infinite set of invented keys. What cannot be derived — a mapper needing a field shape a probe can't fake — lands in `underivable` with a reason and is **shown in the UI**, because a silently missing type reads as "nothing maps to this" rather than "we couldn't tell". It only costs the _state→inventory_ direction: with a real resource in hand the real mapper runs, so matching still works.
- **Two document shapes, version-checked rather than assumed.** `client-core/src/iac/state.ts` reads a raw `.tfstate` (**format version 4** — `resources[].instances[].attributes`) and `terraform show -json` (**format_version 1.x** — `values.root_module`, recursing `child_modules[]`). Version 3 and version 5 are both refused with an explanation; a half-read state would report genuinely managed resources as ClickOps, which is exactly the wrong answer. A plan document (`planned_values`, no `values`) gets its own message. Every bound lives here because the input is untrusted: 8 MiB, 10 000 instances, 250 attributes each, 4 000 chars per value, **64 levels of nesting**, 50 000 nodes per measurement — each enforced _ahead of_ the work it guards (document size on the raw text before `JSON.parse`, via `TextEncoder` byte length rather than code units; the rest incrementally, short-circuiting), never by measuring the whole thing and asking afterwards. The depth bound is the one that is about _shape_ rather than volume, and it exists because the old size measurement was `JSON.stringify`, which recurses: a size-compliant document nesting ~20 000 deep overflowed the stack and escaped as a `RangeError`, i.e. an HTTP 500. `measureStructure` now walks an explicit stack, depth-first so a deep chain trips in as many steps as it is deep. Note V8's `JSON.parse` is _iterative_ and swallows such documents happily — only stringify blows up, so the bug lived entirely in the measurement.
- **Parse-failure translation is total, not an allow-list.** `saveIacState` turns **any** throw from the parse stage into a 400 (`TerraformStateParseError` keeps its message; anything else is logged as a parser gap and still answers 400 with a useful description). Matching on known error classes is precisely what let the `RangeError` through, and the status is not a detail here: a 500 says "we broke", which invites a blind retry of a file that will never work, where a 400 says "this document is not acceptable". Parsing is the trust boundary, so the translation belongs at the boundary rather than in a growing list of recognised types.
- **Secrets: the document is parsed and thrown away.** Only the projection is stored. `sensitive_attributes` (cty paths — the first `get_attr` step names the top-level key) and `sensitive_values` (a `true`-leaf mirror) are both honoured, and a nested sensitive leaf redacts the **whole** top-level attribute — half-redacted structures invite mistakes. Redacted keys survive as names in `redactedAttributeKeys` and are then **filtered out of the diff**, so a secret's value cannot leak through a drift row either. The audit entry for an upload carries counts, never values. **`redactedAttributeKeys` is "values we did not store", not "secrets"** — a clamped value (a truncated string, an oversized structure replaced by `IAC_OMITTED`) goes in the same list, because a value we changed is a value that must never be compared or the storage limit itself reads as drift. The two are counted apart (`redactedAttributeCount` = sensitive, `omittedAttributeCount` = too large) since only one of them is a thing to be careful with, and both are surfaced as parse warnings.
- **The drift comparison is `diffResourceRecords`** — the change-timeline differ, not a second implementation. The state attribute bag plays `prior`, the attributes the export mapper produces from live inventory play `fetched`; the differ walks the keys of the _incoming_ bag, which is exactly the set of attributes the mapper claims to know, so provider bookkeeping in a state entry produces no noise. `normalizeComparableValue` coerces numeric strings on both sides first (`"8"` vs `8` is not drift). The renderer is shared too: `ChangeDiffList` was split into `ResourceFieldDiffList({ diff })` + a thin `ChangeDiffList({ entry })` wrapper so both surfaces render one field diff one way.
- **Matching is identity-first and refuses coincidences.** Candidates in order: the mapper's `importId`, then `externalId`, then — only when the plugin gives no Terraform type — an `id`/`arn`/`self_link` that resolves to **exactly one** unconsumed state entry. Ambiguity is not a match. Every row reports `matchedBy` so the classification can be argued with. A matched resource no plugin can express is `managed` with an `unmappableReason` and counted in `summary.undiffable` — its drift is **unknown**, never reported as absent.
- **Four categories, not three.** Resources in state with no inventory match are their own list (`stateOnly`), split by whether any plugin maps that Terraform type at all — "your account isn't connected" and "nothing here maps `acme_widget`" are different problems.
- **The payoff is `import` blocks, generated by the existing pipeline.** `plugin-base/terraform-export.ts` gained `renderTerraformImportBlocks` + `exportResourcesForAdoption`; the document is Terraform 1.5+ `import {}` blocks followed by the stanzas `exportResourcesToTerraform` already produced. Declarative rather than N `terraform import` commands: the whole set comes under management in one reviewed apply.
- **The adoption invariant: a resource is declared only if it is also imported.** A stanza with no matching `import` block is a **create**, so a document advertised as "paste this and run `terraform plan`" that contains one either fails with already-exists or builds a second copy of a resource the user already has. `exportResourcesForAdoption` therefore drops any mapped resource whose plugin gave no `importId` and reports it in `unsupported` with `NO_IMPORT_ID_REASON` — the same "say so, don't guess" rule as `underivable`. This is why adoption is its own function rather than a renderer over a `TerraformExportOutcome`: the decision must happen **before** the bundle is rendered, so it re-exports the filtered set rather than post-processing (name deduplication is computed over the rendered set — a survivor must not keep a `_2` suffix it only needed to avoid a collision with something now absent). Plain eject-to-Terraform is unaffected: it declares without importing on purpose, and its comment hints are the right shape for reading one resource.
- **Attribution is the other half.** `server-core/src/iac/service.ts` joins `lookupResourceOwners` and the earliest `created` row in `resource_changes` (only `created` — an `updated` says somebody touched it, not that they made it), scoped to the **unmanaged** rows only, since that is the only place "who made this, and when" is a question. Two extra queries, not one per row.
- **Schema** `server-core/src/db/iac-schema.ts`, migration `0103_iac_reconciliation`: `iac_states` (org + optional account FK, both cascade; format/version/serial/lineage; parse warnings; uploader FK `set null`) and `iac_managed_resources` (state FK cascade, address/type/name/index key, `identifiers`, the redacted `attributes` bag). Retention rides the poller's hourly pass beside `pruneResourceChanges`: 20 documents per org on upload, 90 days after that — but `pruneIacStates` **always keeps the newest per org+account scope** (`DISTINCT ON`), because a quiet org must not silently lose its answer to "what does Terraform manage?".
- **API** `web/src/api/routes/iac.ts` — `GET|POST /iac/states`, `DELETE /iac/states/:id`, `GET /iac/reconciliation?stateId=`, `POST /iac/import-plan`, `GET /iac/resource?resourceId=` (a query param, because composite resource ids contain slashes — the `/changes/resource` rule). New permissions **`iac:read` / `iac:write`**: read is in the Member role for the same reason the change timeline is, write is not because uploading a state is the org declaring its estate. API_VERSION 1.10.0 → **1.15.0**.
- **Surfaces** — workspace-tab kind **`iac`**, title "IaC", full chain (ui.store union + three switches, `iacTabTarget`, both platforms' `lib/workspace-tabs.ts`, both viewports, two route stubs, desktop `validateWorkspaceTab` + web `validate-tabs` + the openapi `TabTarget` enum). Panel is shared: `ui/src/iac/IacPanel.tsx` + `IacImportPlanModal` behind a host-injected `IacClient` (web wraps `api*`, desktop wraps six new `cloud_iac_*` IPCs) — the `ChangesClient` arrangement. Desktop is cloud-gated in the panel, so a restored tab in local mode explains instead of fetching.
- **Deferred:** pulling state from a git repo through the existing GitHub App (the natural follow-up — this feature's input is a file today), a CLI subcommand, mobile, and the resource-detail badge's UI (the `GET /iac/resource` endpoint that backs it exists and is spec'd; nothing renders it yet).

## Terraform provider (`terraform-provider-infrawrench/`, standalone Go module)

Manages Infrawrench's **own** configuration as Terraform resources — 46 resources, 6 data sources, each with a per-object plan, drift detection and `terraform import`. Distinct from the three other "Terraform" features (see the naming table under "IaC reconciliation"): eject-to-Terraform writes HCL for the _user's cloud resources_, config-as-code moves a whole org as one JSON document, IaC reconciliation reads a user's Terraform state back in. This one is per-object CRUD against the org-scoped routes.

- **Why not wrap config as code.** That document addresses entities by a name-derived `key` with no server id, so `terraform import` is impossible and a rename plans destroy-and-recreate; apply is whole-document and transactional, which loses writes under Terraform's default parallelism of ten; and `replace` mode deletes anything the document doesn't name, so removing one budget would require owning every budget. It also carries only a handful of the object types the provider manages. Full argument in the provider README.
- **Two-file wire layer.** `internal/iw/wire.go` (cost allocation and reporting) + `wire_platform.go` (monitoring, governance, access, delivery) hold every struct; `endpoints.go` + `endpoints_platform.go` hold every typed method. **Nothing under `internal/provider/` assembles a URL or a body** — that package boundary is the invariant; the file split is readability. Hand-written rather than generated because the SDK generator collapses `discriminator` unions to `any` and drops `readOnly`/`default`, which are exactly the signals deciding whether a Terraform attribute is `Computed`.
- **Three omitempty conventions, and they are load-bearing.** A pointer _with_ `omitempty` means "omitted clears it" (most PUT full-replaces). A pointer _without_ means nil marshals as an explicit `null`, because the server distinguishes absent from null — cost centre `parentId`, account `bastionId`, metric-alert selectors. And where a route is a genuine PATCH (accounts, roles, Slack channels, Teams webhooks, deploy triggers) an omitted key means "leave alone", the opposite of the PUT case, so `Client.Patch` is kept visibly distinct from `Client.Put` rather than papered over.
- **Strict `oneOf` unions get custom marshallers.** `CostDateRange`, `CostExportDestination`, `AlertDestination` and `AlertCondition` are flattened into one Go struct each — that is what lets Terraform express them as a single repeatable block — with a `MarshalJSON` emitting only the named branch's keys. A push destination carrying a stray `channelId`, or a severity clause carrying an empty `values`, is a 400 rather than a field the server ignores. `wire_platform_test.go` pins each branch.
- **Objects with no single-GET route are read by listing and filtering**, and the wrapper synthesises the 404 itself so an out-of-band delete surfaces as "needs recreating" rather than silent success. That is most of them. **Bastions and API keys go further**: DELETE only _revokes_ and the listing keeps returning revoked rows, so `GetBastion`/`GetAPIKey` treat a non-null `revokedAt` as a 404 — without that, `terraform destroy` appears to succeed and the next refresh finds the object still there.
- **Org singletons destroy differently, on purpose.** `infrawrench_network_flow_settings` is the sharpest case: destroy turns collection **off**, because collection runs flow-log queries the provider bills to the customer's own cloud account and a no-op would keep spending their money for a resource they deleted. There is always exactly one settings row, so `Delete` cannot remove anything. Cost settings and alert routing **restore the shipped defaults** (an org routing nothing is worse than the default); currency **clears the display currency** but leaves the rate table; session recording is deliberately a **no-op** (silently disabling an audit control because a block was deleted is not a safe default); drift/expiry/posture are no-ops for lack of documented defaults. They import under the org id via `importOrgSingleton`.
- **Alert routing is one resource holding the whole ordered list**, not one per rule, because order _is_ the semantics (first-match-wins unless `continue_on_match`). Per-rule resources would have needed an invented position attribute and a way to stop two configs claiming a slot. Each rule's id is sent back on every write so in-flight held and escalating deliveries keep pointing at their rule.
- **Write-only fields are carried forward from prior state on every read** — account credentials, API key secret, SSH private key, bastion token, Teams webhook URL, Jira/Linear tokens, deploy answers. No route returns them, so a refresh that didn't carry them would empty them out of state. The provider cannot detect drift on any of them, and says so in each schema. `infrawrench_api_key`, generated `infrawrench_ssh_key` and `infrawrench_bastion` write a credential into state in plaintext because the API returns it once — documented as a state-backend warning rather than hidden.
- **Two client-side normalisations exist to stop permanent diffs.** `exchange_rate.rate` is a decimal string in a `numeric(20,10)` column, so `"1.085"` reads back `"1.0850000000"` — `sameDecimal` (a `big.Rat` compare) keeps the practitioner's spelling when the number is equal and takes the server's when it isn't. And a report notification writes back only the day field its cadence reads, since the server always returns both from non-null defaulted columns.
- **Three tests are the guard rails.** `wire_spec_test.go` fails when `openapi.json` describes a property no Go struct decodes and no `ignored` entry excuses (one-way; extra Go fields are expected because the spec lags). `provider_test.go` asserts `wantResources`/`wantDataSources`. `documentation_test.go` cross-checks the registry against the README table **and** `website/.../features/terraform-provider.md`, both directions — a resource nobody documented, or a table row for something removed, fails the build.
- **`schemasKnownAbsent` is empty** as of the 1.9.0 spec regeneration (which brought scenario models and billing rules in). The list and its test stay: the next object added ahead of a spec refresh goes there, and the test announces when the refresh catches up.
- **Adding a resource does not bump `API_VERSION`** — the provider is a client and changes no HTTP surface. Changing a route it calls does.
- **Outside every JS toolchain**, on purpose: not in `pnpm-workspace.yaml`, `turbo.json`, or `cliff.toml`'s `include_paths`. `cd terraform-provider-infrawrench && go build ./... && go test ./...`, Go 1.25+. `TF_ACC=1` adds acceptance tests, which need credentials and a scratch org.
- **API keys work; two resources are still closed to them.** `apiKeyOrgMiddleware` widened the org tree, so an `iwk_` key authenticates everywhere the provider reaches — except what `API_KEY_DENY_RULES` closes: `/api-keys` entirely and `/team` writes, i.e. `infrawrench_api_key` and writes to `infrawrench_role`. Those are acts a person performs (a key that mints keys outlives its own revocation; a key should not manufacture authority for others), so the provider explains the 403 rather than working around it — `apiKeyDeniedHint`, alongside `apiKeyUnauthorizedHint` for a 401, which now means the key itself was refused rather than the tree refusing keys. Adding a deny rule over a route the provider calls means updating both hints and the two schema descriptions.
- **Documented bounds are enforced bounds**, checked by `TestDocumentedRangesAreValidated`: it walks the served schema and fails on any attribute whose description states a range or length without a validator. Roughly 45 of them were unenforced before that test existed. Two flavours — most routes _reject_ out-of-range values (so validating converts a failed apply into a plan error), but the probe timings are _clamped_, where an unvalidated value applies fine and then disagrees with the configuration forever.
- **`docs/` is the Registry's documentation tab**, generated by `tfplugindocs` from the schemas and committed — the Registry reads it from the release tag and nowhere else, so a provider published without it renders "Documentation Unavailable" (which `v0.1.1` did). The generator is pinned as a Go tool dependency and CI regenerates and fails on a diff, so a schema change cannot ship documenting the previous version. `examples/` supplies the provider index example, per-resource `resource.tf` and an `import.sh` per resource; the directory is in `.prettierignore` because Prettier and the generator otherwise rewrite each other forever.
- **Two versions have been burnt to packaging**, which is why the publish workflow dry-runs the release before touching the satellite: `v0.1.0` attached the registry manifest without a checksum for it (`release.extra_files` does not feed the checksum step — it needs `checksum.extra_files` too), and `v0.1.1` shipped with no `docs/`. Both looked complete everywhere except at the Registry. `project_name` is also pinned in `.goreleaser.yml`: unpinned it defaults to the repo name, which is right in the satellite by luck and wrong anywhere else.
- **Published through a satellite repository**, `Infrawrench/terraform-provider-infrawrench`, on the SDK pattern: the Registry resolves to a Git URL and demands a dedicated repo named `terraform-provider-{NAME}`, which a monorepo subdirectory cannot satisfy. Monorepo is the direction of truth; the satellite's tree — including the `release.yml` that runs GoReleaser there — is mirrored wholesale from `terraform-provider-infrawrench/`, so nothing in it is hand-maintained. Release ritual is one edit to `VERSION`; the ledger is the monorepo tag `terraform-provider-v<version>`, written last so it means "handed off". The provider versions independently of `API_VERSION` — it is a client. MIT licensed rather than BUSL-1.1, because it is vendored into other people's infrastructure repos.

## Accessibility conventions

Established during the July 2026 a11y sweep (react-doctor Accessibility findings: 78 → 0). Keep new code within these patterns:

- **Modals** — `ui/src/components/Modal.tsx` is a native `<dialog>` (free focus trap/restore, Escape via `cancel`, body scroll-lock). Always pass `ariaLabel` (the dialog's accessible name, usually the visible heading text). Backdrop click-away lives in an effect listener, not a JSX handler — keep it that way (jsx_a11y flags mouse handlers on `<dialog>`). SpotlightSearch uses the same native-dialog pattern with `backdrop:` Tailwind variants.
- **Form fields** — visible `<label>` + `htmlFor`/`id` wired via `useId()` (never hardcoded ids; components can mount twice). Composite pickers (CostFilterRows, KeyValueListEditor…) get a `<span>` caption + `role="group"`/`aria-labelledby`, with each inner control `aria-label`ed. Placeholder text is never the only name. Monaco editors are named via `options.ariaLabel`.
- **Hover-revealed controls** — every `opacity-0 group-hover:opacity-100` must also carry `group-focus-within:opacity-100` (+ `focus-visible:opacity-100` on the focusable element itself).
- **Draggable pills** — dnd-kit ref/listeners/attributes go on an inner native `<button>`; the styled wrapper stays non-interactive so pin/open/action buttons are siblings, not nested interactives (reference: `desktop/src/routes/_account-detail/-ResourcePill.tsx`).
- **Menus** — context menus and dropdowns use `role="menu"`/`menuitem`, `aria-expanded`/`aria-haspopup` on triggers, Escape-to-close with focus restore, ArrowUp/Down roving focus, and Shift+F10/ContextMenu-key as the keyboard right-click (reference: `ui/src/components/OrgSwitcher.tsx`, `desktop/src/components/SidebarAccounts.tsx`).
- **Async status** — loading/error/result-count changes go through `role="status"`/`role="alert"` (sr-only where there's no visible text); terminals mirror connection state into an sr-only status div; charts get `role="img"` + a summary `aria-label` (or `aria-hidden` when purely duplicative).
- **Clickable table rows** — the action goes on a real `<button>` in the first cell (the resource/zone/record name), never on the `<tr>`. A row can be given `tabIndex` and an Enter/Space handler, but there is no row role a screen reader announces as activatable, so it lands as a focus stop with no name and no affordance. The button also removes the whole-row click target, and with it the `stopPropagation` every sibling action (Delete, Dismiss, Restore, File issue) needed to escape it — the row keeps only `hover:bg-*`. Where column one is a badge rather than a name, give the button an `aria-label` (`Open <name>` / `Edit <name>`; see `ChildResourceTable`). References: `ui/src/{dns/DnsSection,expiry/ExpirySection,posture/PostureSection,savings/SavingsSection}.tsx`.
- **`aria-activedescendant` listboxes** (`MultiSelect`, `SpotlightSearch`) — focus stays on the `role="combobox"` input, which owns the entire keyboard path (Arrow/Home/End move the active index, Enter activates, Escape closes). Options carry `role="option"` + `tabIndex={-1}` + `onClick`, and that click is the **mouse half** of the pattern, not the only way in: an `onKeyDown` on an option would never fire, because the option never holds focus. Static linters flag it anyway (`click-events-have-key-events`) — leave the comment that says why. Only `option`/`group` may be children of the listbox: loading/empty/error/no-match copy goes in a sibling `role="status"`, and grouped results get a `role="group"` + `aria-labelledby` per header, or the headers are announced as selectable values.
- **Focusable SVG children** — Firefox and Safari do not paint CSS `outline` on SVG elements, so `theme.css`'s global `:focus-visible` rule is invisible on an SVG `<g role="button" tabIndex={0}>`. Draw the ring in SVG instead: `focus:outline-none` + a `group/…`-scoped `<rect>` revealed by `group-focus-visible/…:opacity-100` (reference: `ui/src/components/graph/DependencyGraphView.tsx`). Do **not** make an SVG backdrop focusable to satisfy a linter — the graph's transparent `<rect>` is `aria-hidden` and mouse-only on purpose, the same legitimate pattern as the modal backdrops, with Escape and a visible **Clear** button as the keyboard equivalent.
- **Skip link** — both shells (`{web,desktop}/src/routes/__root.tsx`) render `sr-only focus:not-sr-only` "Skip to content" as the first tab stop, targeting `<main id="main-content">`. Without it a keyboard user walks the tab strip and the whole sidebar again after every navigation. It is per-shell markup, so it has to be added to both.
- **Theme** — `theme.css` foreground tokens clear WCAG AA (4.5:1) against **all four** surfaces (surface, raised, overlay, sunken) in **both** schemes. `ui/src/__tests__/theme-contrast.test.ts` parses the stylesheet and enforces that, so a value that drifts fails the suite rather than shipping. Global `:focus-visible` outline and a `prefers-reduced-motion` kill-switch live at the bottom of `theme.css`.
- **Colour is a token, never a Tailwind palette class.** `text-red-400`, `text-amber-500`, `text-green-400` and friends are one fixed colour in both schemes, so they can only be legible in one of them: light mode is the default for anyone whose OS is not in dark mode, and against `--color-surface` (#ffffff) `text-red-400` measured 2.77:1 — the app's error text was its least readable text. The August 2026 sweep replaced all 841 such utilities with semantic tokens. Reach for:
  - `text-danger` / `text-warning` / `text-success` / `text-info` — status text, each with a `-strong` variant for the higher-emphasis step (hover, or a label on a tinted chip). `-strong` is _darker_ in light mode and _lighter_ in dark, so "stronger" always means "more contrast"; `text-danger hover:text-danger-strong` is the shape a `text-red-400 hover:text-red-500 dark:text-red-300` triple collapses to.
  - `text-severe` — the severity rung between warning and danger (posture `high`, expiry `critical`, `ALPHA` policies), previously orange.
  - `text-notice` — a non-status highlight (complimentary plan, private interconnect), previously purple.
  - `text-series-1` … `text-series-8` — palettes assigned by _position_ rather than meaning: log-stream labels, environment-diff sides. Eight distinct hues, not a severity ramp. Do not use a status token for a categorical slot, and do not half-migrate an array (a `["text-info", "text-fuchsia-400", …]` list is worse than either end).
  - Because the token adapts on its own, a `dark:text-*` companion is now redundant — delete it rather than porting it.
  - Alpha-tinted backgrounds and borders (`bg-red-500/10`, `border-amber-500/40`) were deliberately left as palette classes: they sit behind text rather than being it, and both schemes read them acceptably.
  - Known remaining gap: `--color-accent` is 4.17:1 on `surface-sunken` in light and 4.05:1 in dark, so it is not yet in the tested set.
  - The mobile app is _not_ affected — `mobile/src/lib/theme.ts` is a dark-only named-token object (`colors.danger`, `colors.success`, …) with no Tailwind palette classes, so there is nothing to mirror.
- **Tables** — every `<th>` carries a `scope`. Column headers in a `<thead>` are `scope="col"`; the app has no row-header tables today, so a `scope="row"` means someone promoted a first-column `<td>` and should say why. A table gets an explicit accessible name **only when nothing adjacent already supplies one**: a `<caption className="sr-only">` where the table stands alone in a panel or a result pane whose only neighbours are a toolbar or a toggle button (query results, the file list, artifacts, secret versions, deploy history, the admin org list), and nothing at all where a visible heading sits immediately above it (`ChildResourceTable`'s `spec.title`, "Deploy on push", "Recent firings", "Lines", "Anomalies", every settings section) — a second announcement of a name the user just heard is noise, not help. Tables whose name is supplied from outside the component get neither: `ChatMarkdown` renders a model-authored table and `SchemaRenderer`'s `TableNode` has no label field. Several `<table>`s are pure row layout with no `<thead>` at all (DNS zones, savings, posture, expiry, the env-diff field lists); they need no `scope` and no caption.
- **Website landmarks** — `app/packages/website` is Astro and shares none of the above CSS. Every page wraps its content in `<main id="main-content" tabindex="-1">` (`index.astro`, `privacy.astro`, and `DocsLayout.astro` for everything under `/docs`), and `Nav.astro` — which is the first thing in `<body>` on every page — opens with `<a class="skip-link" href="#main-content">`. That single placement is what gives the skip link to the whole site; the docs layout is where it earns its keep, since the sidebar `<nav>` is ~60 links deep and precedes the article on every page. `.skip-link` lives in `src/styles/global.css` (offscreen via `transform`, revealed on focus, with a `prefers-reduced-motion` branch) — do not reach for the apps' Tailwind `sr-only`, which that package does not share. Markdown tables in the docs get their `scope="col"`/`scope="row"` from `src/lib/rehype-table-scope.mjs`, wired into `astro.config.mjs` beside `remarkDocs`, so authors never hand-write HTML to get a readable table.

## Code conventions from the July 2026 quality sweep

Nine parallel passes over the whole repo (deduplication, shared types, dead code, circular
imports, weak types, defensive code, legacy paths, comments, godfiles). The rules below are what
those passes established or discovered; the reasoning is preserved because in several places the
obvious-looking cleanup is the wrong one.

### Where a shared type lives

There is no `shared-types` package — do not create one. Types go in exactly one of four places,
chosen by who must agree:

- **`@infrawrench/plugin-base`** — the plugin↔host contract. Zero internal dependencies; nothing
  may make it depend on an app package.
- **`@infrawrench/client-core`** — every HTTP wire shape, plus the pure client logic that operates
  on it (cost contract, dashboard cards, chat, WS frames, push payloads, account-section rules).
  Depends only on `plugin-base`. This is the home for anything the _server produces and a client
  consumes_: `web`, `desktop`, `mobile`, `server-core` and the CLI all reach it.
- **`@infrawrench/ui`** — React-facing types (component props, editor state) plus a re-export shim
  over client-core, because web and desktop depend on `ui` rather than client-core directly.
  `ui/src/api-types.ts` is that shim: add new wire types to `client-core/src/api-types.ts`.
- **the owning package** — anything genuinely local.

`server-core` depends on `client-core`, never the reverse, so a wire contract is declared once and
the producer is typechecked against it. Annotate a route's service function with the client-core
type rather than letting the object literal float — `getOrgCostStatus` went unannotated while three
separate declarations claimed to describe its output.

Prefer deriving over restating: `z.infer`, `$inferSelect`, `Extract<Union, {…}>`, `Omit<…>`. Where
a zod schema and a hand-written type must match, pin them with the `Exact<A, B>` assertion pattern
in `ui/src/cost/config.ts` — it fails the build on drift and costs nothing at runtime.

**Mobile is the tell.** If a type lives in `ui` and mobile needs it, mobile _cannot_ import it and
will silently hand-write a second copy. That is how the mobile billing screen came to read
`GET /billing/status` as a bare subscription when the server returns a
`{ complimentary, subscription }` envelope — every org rendered blank plan rows for as long as both
copies existed.

### Module layering and circular imports

The workspace package graph is a DAG and should stay one: `plugin-base` and `client-core` are
leaves, `ui` depends on `client-core` and never the reverse, `server-core` depends on the plugins,
and `web`/`desktop`/`mobile`/`poller` sit on top. Adding an `@infrawrench/*` dependency that points
back down the stack is the one thing that turns a clean graph into a tangled one.

Check with `npx madge --circular --extensions ts,tsx --ts-config tsconfig.json src` from inside a
package (its own tsconfig, so `@/…` aliases resolve), or
`npx dpdm --no-warning --no-tree --exit-code circular:1 'src/**/*.ts'`, which additionally follows
pnpm workspace links. Neither is a devDependency; run them with `npx`.

Where a module has both a contract (types describing a payload) and machinery (DB queries, network
transport), put the contract in its own import-free leaf. `server-core/src/push/` is the pattern:
`types.ts` (leaf) ← `expo-client.ts` (transport) ← `dispatch.ts` (DB fan-out), with `dispatch.ts`
re-exporting the types so the published `push/dispatch` subpath stays stable. This matters more
than the cycle itself — `db/client` opens a connection as an import side effect, so a type living
beside it is a connection waiting to be opened by a consumer who only wanted a string union.

### Error handling

Errors must reach a place a human sees: an HTTP response, a WS error frame, a toast or React error
state, a structured log in a long-running process, or a non-zero CLI exit. A `catch` that returns
`[]`, `null` or `0` turns a backend outage into an empty UI, which is worse than a 500 because
nobody can tell it apart from "you have no data".

- **`isXConfigured()` and "X is broken" are different states.** ClickHouse readers
  (`server-core/src/clickhouse/readers.ts`) return `[]` when ClickHouse is not configured — that
  deployment genuinely has no metric history. A configured-but-failing ClickHouse throws.
- **`Promise.allSettled` is for isolation, not for ignoring.** Wherever it keeps one failure from
  killing a batch (poller type listings, pin probes, notification fan-out), the rejected `reason`
  must still be logged with enough context to identify which item failed.
- Fan-out helpers (`sendSlackToOrg`, `sendMsTeamsToOrg`, `sendPushToOrg`) deliberately return
  delivery counts instead of throwing, and each has a `…Test` counterpart that _does_ throw so the
  settings UI can show the real transport error. Keep that pairing when adding a transport.

### Shared write helpers in `server-core`

Three invariants that used to be copy-pasted now live in one place each:

- **`secret-states`** — `setLiteralSecretState` / `setOutputRefSecretState`. A
  `secret_field_states` row is either a literal or an output-ref, and writing one kind must clear
  the other kind's columns. Adding a column to that table means updating the clear-list in
  `src/secret-states.ts` and nowhere else. (The seven inlined copies had drifted: one flipped
  `resolution_kind` to `output-ref` while leaving `encrypted_value` populated, retaining secret
  material the user believed they had replaced.)
- **`created-resource`** — `upsertCreatedResource`, for the four flows that create a resource and
  need its `resources` row before the next sync. Deliberately distinct from `sync-resources`'s
  `upsertResource`, which merges JSON columns and bumps `sync_version` because a lister's view is
  partial; a create response is authoritative and overwrites.
- **`tick-loop`** — the `TickLoop` base class behind the poller and github-watcher, plus
  `runService` / `installShutdownHandlers`. Ticks are scheduled after the previous one settles,
  never on a fixed interval; an overrunning tick is skipped rather than run concurrently; `stop()`
  allows 30s of drain to match Kubernetes' `terminationGracePeriodSeconds`.

`server-core/env-loader.mjs` is the single dev-time `.env` preloader for web, poller and
github-watcher. It searches `app/packages/web/` first (that package owns the canonical `.env`),
then the cwd, and never overwrites an existing `process.env` entry.

`@infrawrench/ui`'s `src/components/schema-tokens.ts` owns the mapping from a plugin's
`BadgeNode.color` / `StatusDotNode.status` to Tailwind classes. Renderers must not inline their own
ladder — the peer pane and the detail view previously drew `degraded` in two different yellows.

### Typing vendor SDK request bodies

Plugin clients assemble create/update bodies from a `Record<string, string>` of form fields.
Annotate the body with the SDK's own params type (`const body: XCreateParams = {…}`) — never build
a `Record<string, unknown>` and cast. The cast is not free: it hides the case where a free-text
form field is posted against a closed string-literal union, which is a silent API 400. Removing 36
such casts from the Cloudflare plugin surfaced 17 live wire-format bugs. When a form value must
become a literal union, narrow it with a membership guard and a documented default:

```ts
const METRICS = ["cosine", "euclidean", "dot-product"] as const;
type Metric = (typeof METRICS)[number];
const isMetric = (v: string): v is Metric => (METRICS as readonly string[]).includes(v);
```

A second cast is not a fix. If the params type is a discriminated union the body genuinely cannot
satisfy (Cloudflare DNS records, Page Rules actions), keep the cast and name the union and the
`.d.ts` line in a comment.

- **Hand-written ambient module declarations are unverified.** `.d.ts` files we author for untyped
  dependencies (e.g. `memcached/src/memjs.d.ts`) are asserted, not checked — a wrong signature
  there is worse than no types, because call sites work around it with casts instead of fixing it.
  That file promised a `stats(): Promise<…>` that memjs does not have. Read the dependency's source
  before declaring a method.
- **`tsc` (TypeScript 7 / native) historically did not surface mixin-provided members** (`class X extends Mixin(Base) {}`). mysql2's
  `query`/`execute` are the live example, so those casts are load-bearing — build the local
  interface out of the library's own exported packet types so a breaking upgrade still shows up.
- **`exactOptionalPropertyTypes`** is inherited by every package including `ui`. Widen the
  _declaration_ to `prop?: T | undefined` when the type is ours; use a conditional spread
  `{...(v !== undefined ? { k: v } : {})}` when the receiving type is a third party's.

### Dead-code tooling

`knip.json` drives the sweep (`npx knip`). Two things about it are load-bearing:

- **Entry points must match the real build inputs, not the obvious file.** The desktop main-process
  entry is `electron/index.ts` (the `--cli` dispatcher), _not_ `electron/main.ts`. Pointing knip at
  `main.ts` makes it report the entire `electron/cli/` tree — the shipped CLI — as dead code.
- **`@kubernetes/client-node`, `cloudflare`, `dockerode` and `ssh2` are deliberately direct
  dependencies** of `web`, `poller` and `github-watcher` even though no source file imports them.
  Each esbuild `build` script marks them `--external:`, so they must resolve from `node_modules` at
  runtime, reached through the plugin node drivers. They sit in `ignoreDependencies` — do not
  "clean them up".

Knip's `exports` and `types` buckets are noisy here: a type referenced only by an exported
signature in its own module still counts as unused. Treat that bucket as informational.

### TypeScript 7 + tsdown

TypeScript 7 ships the Go-native compiler as `tsc` (platform binaries under `@typescript/typescript-*`). The monorepo pins `typescript@^7.0.2` everywhere for typecheck/build.

Library packages build with **tsdown** (not tsup). tsup's DTS path still depends on the classic TS JS API and cannot run on pure TS 7; tsdown emits types via the native compiler and peers `typescript@^5 || ^6 || ^7`. Configs live in `tsdown.config.ts` and use `outExtensions` so output names stay `index.js` / `index.cjs` / `index.d.ts` / `index.d.cts` (matching the previous tsup layout). `unrun` is an optional peer used whenever Node lacks type stripping; keep it as a root `devDependency` so EAS (and any Node < 22.6) can still load those configs — see Mobile CI.

TS 7 does **not** ship a stable JS compiler API yet (expected in 7.1). The few places that still need `transpileModule` / `createProgram` / `ts.sys` import `@typescript/typescript6` explicitly (or alias `typescript` to it):

- `workflow-runtime` (runtime transpile + headless typecheck + `copy-ts-libs.mjs`)
- `web` SDK generator (`scripts/sdk/targets/typescript`)
- `website` keeps `typescript` aliased to `@typescript/typescript6` for `@astrojs/check` (peer is still `^5 || ^6`)
- `mobile` keeps the same alias: Expo CLI's `evaluateTsConfig` does `ts.sys.getCurrentDirectory` when Metro starts (`expo export:embed` on EAS), and pure TS 7 leaves `ts.sys` undefined — fails with `Cannot read properties of undefined (reading 'getCurrentDirectory')`. Bumping Expo does not help until it stops requiring the classic JS API.

`tsconfig.base.json` sets `types: ["*"]` (TS 6/7 default is `[]`). Do **not** reintroduce `@typescript/native-preview` / `tsgo` / tsup.

**`noUnusedLocals` is not enabled in any tsconfig**, so dead imports and locals are invisible to
`pnpm typecheck`. A periodic `tsc --noEmit --noUnusedLocals -p <pkg>/tsconfig.json` sweep is the
only thing that catches them; it found 123 where knip found one unused file in ~1,830.

### Decomposing big plugin clients

Split by vendor domain, not by line count. The established shape (AWS, and now DigitalOcean) is a
thin `PluginClient` class owning auth, caches and dispatch, delegating to ctx-object modules beside
it: `create-handlers.ts` (dispatcher) + `create-handlers/<domain>.ts`, `detail-renderers.ts`
(barrel) + `detail-renderers/<domain>.ts`, `resource-listers.ts`, `metric-series.ts`,
`enrich-detail.ts`, `status-dots.ts`, `nosql-console.ts`. Each extracted module takes a narrow
context interface holding just the `fetch` wrapper and the caches it needs, so modules stay
leaf-ward of the client and unit-test with a fake `fetch`. The original module path always keeps
exporting the same names, so a split is never a breaking change.

`create-handlers/` modules return `null` for a `typeId` they do not handle; the dispatcher takes
the first non-null result and throws if every module declines. Arms are mutually exclusive, so
module order is not significant.

A big file is not automatically a godfile. `cloudflare/src/metric-series.ts` is ~1,750 lines of one
export over 12 homogeneous per-product fetchers — splitting it buys a 13-import barrel and nothing
else. `desktop/src/lib/agent-client.ts` is multi-responsibility but knitted together by six
module-level singleton maps, where a wrong split is a silent duplicate-VM-provision bug.

### Legacy paths deliberately kept

- **`organization_members.role` / `invitations.role` text fallback.** Migration `0009` added
  `role_id` with no backfill; it is populated lazily by `backfillMembershipRole()`, so production
  still has `role_id IS NULL` rows where the text fallback is the only thing resolving permissions.
- **API-key legacy SHA-256 lookup / `legacyHashSunsetAt`** — an active self-terminating sunset, not
  stale compat. Removing it locks out keys still sitting in customers' CI.
- **The `{ role }` body param and the `resourceIds` reorder form** — superseded for first-party
  clients but published in `openapi.json` and all nine SDKs, so removal is a major bump.
- **v1 ciphertext reads, desktop `master.key`, GCP legacy Cloud Run ids, AWS `legacyHost()`** — all
  read data at rest or are the only implementation of a live path.

`APP_URL` is now the only app-origin env var; the `NEXT_PUBLIC_APP_URL` fallback (a leftover from
the pre-Hono Next.js shell) is gone. `PUBLIC_BASE_URL` still takes precedence for the OpenAPI
server URL and the MCP well-known documents only. `invitations.token` was dropped in migration
`0035_concerned_mesmero` — invitations are hash-only at rest, the raw token existing only in the
one-time `POST /team/invitations` response. `decrypt()` now always takes an AAD; desktop's
`main-utils.ts` keeps `aad` optional because it still writes v1 for cloud tokens.

### Known-broken, recorded rather than fixed

- **Chat metered billing has no replay path.** `web/src/chat/billing.ts` reports each `chat_usage`
  row to Stripe best-effort. If Stripe is unreachable the row keeps a null `stripeUsageRecordId`
  and is never retried — that usage is not billed. `chat_usage_unreported_idx` exists to support a
  replay job that has not been written. Do not assume unreported rows are eventually reconciled.
- **Fallback rows must not invent field values.** When a lister's `Describe*` enrichment fails,
  push empty strings for the fields you could not read (see the ACM fallback in
  `aws/src/resource-listers-extended/security.ts`), never a plausible default. The WAF, Cognito and
  Step Function fallbacks in that file violate this and will display `defaultAction: ALLOW` /
  `mfaConfiguration: OFF` for resources whose real posture is unknown.
- **Mobile secret fields render a "Tap to copy" affordance wired to nothing** — the
  `resolveFieldValue` handler it dispatches to is supplied by no screen, so it silently no-ops.
- **`FilestoreInstanceResourceType`** is fully implemented in the GCP plugin — lister, create
  config, create, delete, resolve-output, tests — and simply never registered in the manifest.

## Weekly digest

The weekly org summary (spend WoW + movers, sync incidents, resource churn) lives in
`server-core/src/digest/`: `compose.ts` is pure (window math, schedule math, totals/movers, all
three transports' formatting — unit-tested without mocks in `__tests__/digest-compose.test.ts`),
`weekly.ts` does the gathering, claiming, delivery and retry bookkeeping, `narrative.ts` is the
optional LLM paragraph, `recipients.ts` the email address list. Scheduling is the poller's fourth
tick pass (`tickDigests` in `poller/src/loop.ts`). Exactly-once across replicas/restarts comes from
one conditional UPDATE on `org_digest_settings.last_sent_week_start` — only rows the UPDATE moved
forward are returned to send, and **that column never moves backwards**, which is what keeps the
invariant true across timezone changes (`GREATEST(...)` in the settings upsert enforces it).
Delivery reuses `sendSlackToOrg`/`sendMsTeamsToOrg` with the fifth channel trigger `weeklyDigest`
(`ChannelTrigger` in `push/types.ts` — deliberately _not_ a `PushTrigger`; the digest never goes to
mobile push), plus email. Sync incidents are counted from `paging_incidents`, not
`account_sync_failures` — the failure table is a rolling window the pager prunes within minutes and
skips entirely while paging is off, so it cannot answer "how many last week". Enabling the digest
stamps `last_sent_week_start` to the current window so the first scheduled send is the next send
time, not the moment the toggle flips.

**Per-org schedule and the week-boundary math** (migration `0053`). `org_digest_settings` gained
`timezone` (IANA, default `UTC`), `send_day` (ISO 1–7, default 1) and `send_hour` (0–23, default 7),
so every pre-existing org keeps Monday 07:00 UTC exactly. The whole approach is: **work in civil
values, never instants.** `civilMoment(now, tz)` reads the wall clock via `Intl.DateTimeFormat`
(`hourCycle: "h23"`, `formatToParts`) into `{date, hour, isoDow}`; `addDays` shifts a `YYYY-MM-DD`
by whole days through `Date.UTC` used purely as a calendar calculator; `digestWindow` derives the
Mon–Sun window from the local date; `isDigestDue` compares `(localDate, localHour)` against
`(dueDate, sendHour)` lexicographically. **DST is therefore structurally irrelevant rather than
patched around** — a week is always seven _dated_ days whatever their length in ms, a spring-forward
skipped send hour is reached the moment the clock clears it (late, never skipped), and a fall-back
repeated hour is absorbed by the claim. Non-hour offsets (`Asia/Kolkata` +05:30) and date-line zones
(`Pacific/Kiritimati` +14, `Pacific/Niue` −11) need no special case; all are unit-tested. No
timezone library was added — `Intl` already ships the tz database, and the repo had no date
dependency to reuse. Zones are validated with a `new Intl.DateTimeFormat({timeZone})` try/catch
(an allowlist would go stale); the API rejects unknown zones, and `civilMoment` falls back to UTC
rather than throwing so one bad row can't stop every other org's digest. `send_day` chooses when the
message _arrives_, not what a week _is_ — a Wednesday digest still recaps Mon–Sun. Known
approximation, unchanged from the UTC-only era: local window dates are matched against UTC day
buckets in ClickHouse/Postgres, which is as fine as provider billing exports get.

**Retry/park semantics — no claim rollback.** PR #22's candidate fix ("revert the claim on total
delivery failure") was rejected: reverting lets two replicas race back into the same slot. Instead
the claim stays monotonic and the retry is **its own claimable gate**. New columns:
`attempt_count`, `last_attempt_at`, `last_status`, `last_error`, `next_attempt_at`.
`claimRetryDigestOrgs` is a single UPDATE that requires `last_status = 'failed'` and an elapsed
`next_attempt_at`, and **nulls `next_attempt_at` in the same statement** — that null is the claim, so
concurrent replicas can't both take a retry. `MAX_DIGEST_ATTEMPTS` = 3 with 15/60-minute backoff,
then parked until next week. The classification is the load-bearing part (`classifyDelivery`):
`succeeded` (all landed), **`partial` (some landed) is never retried** — a resend would post the
digest into Slack twice — `failed` (nothing landed, and build errors) is retryable, and
`no_targets` (nothing routed at all) is surfaced but not retried. That classification is the _only_
thing preventing a double-delivery, and it is enough: `failed` means zero destinations succeeded,
address-level email fan-out included, so there is nothing to duplicate. Do not weaken it on the
assumption the mail provider dedups — Mailgun has no idempotency keys (a long-standing open feature
request). Each message does carry `v:infrawrench-key` =
`digest:<org>:<weekStart>:<scheduled|manual-…>:<addr>`, but that is a Mailgun custom variable for
tracing a send in their logs, not a dedup token; it is named `traceKey` in the code to keep that
honest. `last_sent_at` now moves only when a digest
actually reached someone — claiming no longer stamps it, which it used to. All of this is exposed
through `GET /digest` and rendered in `WeeklyDigestSection`, deliberately: KNOWLEDGE's standing note
about poller error invisibility applies here, and a digest that silently stopped arriving is the
same failure class. **Mobile renders the same status block** (`settings/notifications.tsx` →
`WeeklyDigestSection` / `digestStatusView`), for exactly that reason, alongside the enable switch,
the `narrativeEnabled` opt-in and Send now; the schedule and the recipient list are read-only there
(see the mobile omissions above). The whole section 403s to a non-admin and simply renders nothing —
`GET /digest` takes `org:settings:write`, so the phone needs no separate permission check. Tested end-to-end in `__tests__/digest-retry.test.ts` against an in-memory DB
fake whose `drizzle-orm` mock turns predicates into functions, so the real WHERE clauses are
exercised rather than stubbed.

**Email is the third transport, and the first email in the codebase.** `server-core/src/email.ts`,
Mailgun's `POST {base}/v3/{domain}/messages` over plain `fetch` — zero new dependencies, same
env-gated no-op shape as Slack. Basic auth (`api:<key>`) and a `multipart/form-data` body built with
the runtime's own `FormData`, which is what Mailgun documents; do **not** set `Content-Type` by hand,
as that strips the boundary `fetch` generates. **There is no Google-native option to migrate to** —
GCP has no first-party transactional mail service, its own "sending email from an instance" guide
names Mailgun/SendGrid/Mailjet, and outbound port 25 is blocked, so a hosted provider over HTTPS is
the only shape that works from the cluster; Workspace SMTP relay would need a Workspace subscription
and an SMTP client dependency. WorkOS was _not_ reused: it composes and sends its own
verification/invitation mail and exposes no general send API. Env: `MAILGUN_API_KEY` +
`MAILGUN_DOMAIN` + `EMAIL_FROM` all required together, plus optional `MAILGUN_API_BASE` for
EU-region accounts (a different host, not a path); all in `.env.example` and the tfvars `app_env`
map; unset ⇒ logged no-op and
the digest still reaches Slack/Teams. One request per address (a shared `to:` would leak the list and
one bounce would take the whole send). Recipients are an **org-level address list**
(`digest_email_recipients`), _not_ a per-member opt-in: the digest is a `ChannelTrigger`, so its
destinations are admin-configured like a Slack channel, and an address list also reaches a `finance@`
alias with no Infrawrench login. `push_preferences` remains the precedent for the four _alert_
triggers only. Email gets its own renderers (`formatDigestEmailText` / `formatDigestEmailHtml`) — the
Slack body's mrkdwn asterisks would render literally and a mail client wants both parts; the HTML is
hand-rolled with inline styles (mail clients strip `<style>`), no templating dependency, and escapes
everything outside the `<strong>` spans `digestLines` produced.

**AI narrative** (`digest/narrative.ts`, opt-in via `org_digest_settings.narrative_enabled`, default
off). `@anthropic-ai/sdk` was already a `web` dependency for chat but the digest runs in the
**poller**, so it was added to `server-core` too (same version, already in the lockfile). Model is
`claude-opus-5` with `output_config: {effort: "low"}`, `max_tokens` 512, 30s timeout, `maxRetries: 1`.
**Every** failure path returns `null` — unconfigured, HTTP error, timeout, empty completion,
`stop_reason: "refusal"` — and the digest sends its deterministic content regardless; there is no
code path here that can stop a digest. `compose.ts` stays pure and never imports it: `weekly.ts`
calls it between composing and delivering and threads the paragraph back as an opaque string
(`digestLines(digest, bold, narrative?)`). `digestPromptPayload` is an **allowlist written field by
field** over `WeeklyDigest`, not a `JSON.stringify` of it, so a later addition to the digest type
cannot widen what leaves the deployment by accident — the model sees currency totals, WoW deltas,
mover names with spend, and three counts. Nothing else. `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` is shared with chat and
is now documented in both env files.

## Two-way Slack (slash commands + approval buttons)

Slack went from write-only to two-way. Outbound stays as it was; the public inbound endpoints
live in `web/src/api/routes/slack-inbound.ts`, mounted at the API root next to the OAuth
callback: `POST /api/slack/commands` (the `/infrawrench` slash command), `POST
/api/slack/interactions` (`block_actions` from message buttons), and `GET` + `POST
/api/slack/link` (the browser half of account linking — session-authed, bounces through sign-in
with `return_to`; the GET only renders a confirmation page, the double-submit-CSRF-guarded POST
writes the link). All are `x-internal` in the published spec (`public-spec.ts` `INTERNAL_PATHS`).
Both Slack POST endpoints ack with an empty 200 _before_ doing any real work — Slack cuts them
off at 3 seconds and a cost query can be slower — and deliver all feedback through
`response_url` / `chat.update`.

**Auth is three layers, in order, and each exists for a reason.** (1) Every POST is verified
against `SLACK_SIGNING_SECRET` (`verifySlackRequestSignature` in `server-core/src/slack.ts`:
HMAC-SHA256 over `v0:<ts>:<raw body>`, constant-time compare, 5-minute replay window — the _raw_
body, so read `c.req.text()` before any parsing). The secret gates the whole inbound half:
`isSlackInboundConfigured()` false ⇒ 503 for everything, while outbound alerts keep working.
(2) The Slack `user_id` must resolve through `slack_user_links` (new table: unique per
(org, team, Slack user)) to a **current** org member — the resolver inner-joins
`organization_members`, so a leaver's link dies on its own. Unknown users get an ephemeral link
prompt carrying a signed 15-minute token (`signSlackLinkToken`, keyed off the signing secret so
rotation voids outstanding tokens); `GET /api/slack/link` verifies it against the _session's_
user, requires membership of the token's org, and shows a confirmation page naming the Slack
user and org — the upsert only happens on the `POST`, which additionally requires the
`slack_link_csrf` cookie/form pair the GET minted. One Slack workspace can be installed
into several orgs; links are per-org and commands fan across every linked org. (3) Each surface
re-checks the same permission as its web twin via `resolveEffectivePermissions`: `costs:read`
(`/infrawrench costs` — summary built on the same `runCostQuery` the dashboard uses),
`resources:read` (`/infrawrench status <name>`, fuzzy `ilike`+score matching with a button picker
on ambiguity), `workflows:approve` (workflow approval buttons), and chat-conversation ownership +
`chat:write` (agent tool approval buttons).

**Buttons never create approvals; they only decide existing rows through the web UI's own code
paths** — `decideWorkflowApproval`'s conditional UPDATE (losers get "already decided"), and the
chat pending-action transitions: both the Slack handler and the web decision route claim
`pending → approved/rejected` with a conditional UPDATE ... RETURNING, so racing deciders across
either surface produce exactly one decision (the loser gets an ack/409). The Slack copy
of every approval request now goes out via `sendSlackToOrgTracked` (returns per-channel
`{installationId, channelId, ts}`), refs are stored in `slack_approval_messages` (new table,
`kind` "workflow"|"chat"), and `updateSlackApprovalMessages`
(`server-core/src/slack-approvals.ts`) rewrites _every_ copy — buttons removed, "✅ Approved by X
via Slack/the web app" context, threaded reply — on any decision, web included (hooked inside
`decideWorkflowApproval` and the chat routes, fire-and-forget, never throws). Expiry retires the
copies too (`expirePending`'s winning claim, and the late-approval conflict path inside
`decideWorkflowApproval`, both render a "⏳ Expired" outcome), and
`recordSlackApprovalMessages` re-reads the approval after inserting refs so a decision that
landed mid-post still converges. Slack messages never carry raw tool input — `chatApprovalText`
summarizes field _names_ only; the full input stays in the in-app approval view. Chat tool approvals
additionally mirror into Slack at suspension time (`web/src/chat/slack-approvals.ts`, called from
the destructive branch of `runAgentTurn`), and a Slack decision resumes the agent turn
server-side by draining `runAgentTurn` — that is how the outcome lands back in the conversation
with nobody's browser open. Chat tool execution off a button click is forked after the ack: Slack
demands a 200 within 3 seconds and a destructive tool can run longer.

Gotchas: the `commands` bot scope was added to `SLACK_SCOPES` (existing installs need a reinstall
to get the slash command); the tfvars comment used to _promise_ no signing secret was needed —
that promise is gone, keep the three request URLs (`oauth/callback`, `commands`, `interactions`)
in the Slack app config in step with `APP_URL`; `response_url` is only safe to POST to because
the payload carrying it was signature-verified (do not post to URLs from unverified sources).
Tests: `server-core/__tests__/slack.test.ts` (signature + link tokens + tracked send),
`slack-approvals.test.ts`, and `web/routes/__tests__/slack-inbound.test.ts` (the layer-ordering
and permission matrix).

## Credential preflight + least-privilege policy generator

Generalizes the `CostSetupError` "your credential can't do X, here's the fix" signal to every
capability, at add-account time and on demand. Contract lives in
`plugin-architecture/packages/plugin-base/src/preflight.ts`: a plugin declares
`manifest.preflight.capabilities` (each with provider-native `requiredPermissions` strings +
human labels), implements `PluginClient.verifyCredentials()` (read-only probe → per-capability
ok / missing-with-permissions / unknown), and optionally `Plugin.policyTemplate(capabilityIds)`
(paste-ready least-privilege document; credential-free, so it sits on the Plugin, not the
client). Manifest zod schema validates the declaration (capability ids must be unique); plugins without
it change nothing. `validatePreflightContract` (plugin-base) runs in both plugin loaders:
declaring `preflight.templateFormat` without implementing `policyTemplate()` rejects the plugin
at registration. A missing `verifyCredentials` can't be checked at registration (no client
exists until `createClient` runs with credentials) — the bundled-plugin test harness pins it
down instead, and `runAccountPreflight` degrades it to all-`unknown` checks at probe time.

The host-side runner `runAccountPreflight(plugin, client)` lives in **client-core**
(`preflight.ts`), not server-core, because both hosts execute it: the web server (against
submitted or stored credentials — creds never reach the browser) and the desktop renderer
(local + cloud accounts both resolve credentials renderer-side and run in-process).
`@infrawrench/server-core/preflight` is a re-export. The runner never throws: no declaration ⇒
`supported:false`; thrown probes / skipped capabilities ⇒ `unknown` rows; messages clamped to
600 chars and helpLinks https-only (same discipline as `describeCostFailure`, structural not
instanceof). Pure checklist merging (`buildPreflightChecklist`/`summarizePreflight`) is also in
client-core; the generic UI (`CredentialPreflightPanel`/`CredentialPreflightModal` in
`@infrawrench/ui`) renders declaration + report with zero provider knowledge and is embedded in
the shared `AddAccountModal` (gated on `PluginInfo.preflight`) and behind "Check credentials"
buttons on both account detail pages. Routes: `POST /accounts/preflight` (ad-hoc, pre-create,
validates bastion like account create), `POST /accounts/{id}/preflight`,
`GET /accounts/plugins/{pluginId}/policy-template?capabilities=…` (unknown capability ids are
rejected with 400 — a typo must not silently widen the template to select-all; valid ids are
deduplicated). Nothing is persisted — reports are computed on demand, no schema change.

Flagship implementations (permission names verified against provider docs 2026-08):
**AWS** (`aws/src/preflight.ts`) — `sts:GetCallerIdentity` (needs no permission; failure ⇒ bad
keys) then `iam:SimulatePrincipalPolicy` for exact per-action verdicts (assumed-role ARNs map
back to the role; `:root` short-circuits to all-ok), falling back to one cheap probe per
capability when the simulator is denied (the CE probe costs ~$0.01, fallback only). Template:
IAM policy JSON, per-capability Sids + always-on `iam:SimulatePrincipalPolicy`. Note
`parseXml` unwraps the response root — read `SimulatePrincipalPolicyResult` at top level.
**GCP** (`gcp/src/preflight.ts`) — one `cloudresourcemanager v3 projects:testIamPermissions`
POST (raw fetch; the GcpClient `get` helper is GET-only), granted-subset diff; blank
`billingExportTable` downgrades costs to missing with the export-setup helpLink while keeping
any IAM permissions the probe already found missing. Template: custom role YAML for
`gcloud iam roles create --file` (no wildcards allowed in custom roles); the `resources` set
spells out one verified list permission per lister in `client.ts listResources` — note the
instance-group lister reads aggregated **instanceGroupManagers**, so the permission is
`compute.instanceGroupManagers.list`, and the KMS/Tasks/Scheduler listers fan out via
ListLocations, which has its own `*.locations.list` permission.
**Cloudflare** (`cloudflare/src/preflight.ts`) — `/user/tokens/verify` (account-owned tokens
are rejected there with code 1000 — treated as "can't verify", not invalid; non-active status
flags every row), then per-capability probes: `/zones` + `/accounts` (Zone Read / Account
Settings Read), GraphQL `viewer.budget` (Analytics Read), `/accounts/{id}/billable-usage/info`
(Billing Read). Every probe is three-way: ok only on explicit success, missing only on
401/403/auth-coded envelope errors, anything else (5xx, malformed body) is `unknown` with the
provider's message — a server error is not evidence a scope is granted or missing. Template:
text permission-group list + the documented
`dash.cloudflare.com/profile/api-tokens?permissionGroupKeys=…` deep link (same mechanism as the
manifest's create-token helpLink).
